Federated Stochastic Approximation under Markov Noise and Heterogeneity: Applications in Reinforcement Learning
Sajad Khodadadianlabel=e1, mark]sajadk@vt.edu, Pranay Sharmalabel=e2, mark]pranaysh@andrew.cmu.edu, Gauri Joshilabel=e3, mark]gaurij@andrew.cmu.edu, Siva Theja Magulurilabel=e4, mark]siva.theja@gatech.edu

Abstract
Since reinforcement learning algorithms are notoriously data-intensive, the task of sampling observations from the environment is usually split across multiple agents. However, transferring these observations from the agents to a central location can be prohibitively expensive in terms of communication cost, and it can also compromise the privacy of each agent’s local behavior policy. Federated reinforcement learning is a framework in which N N agents collaboratively learn a global model, without sharing their individual data and policies . This global model is the unique fixed point of the average of N N local operators, corresponding to the N N agents. Each agent maintains a local copy of the global model and updates it using locally sampled data. In this paper, we show that by careful collaboration of the agents in solving this joint fixed point problem, we can find the global model N N times faster, also known as linear speedup. We first propose a general framework for federated stochastic approximation with Markovian noise and heterogeneity, showing linear speedup in convergence. We then apply this framework to federated reinforcement learning algorithms, examining the convergence of federated on-policy TD, off-policy TD, and Q Q -learning.

1
Introduction

Stochastic Approximation (SA)
[
68
]
is a fundamental concept in the field of optimization and computational mathematics. It refers to a family of iterative methods used to find the roots of functions when the function itself cannot be directly observed but can be estimated via noisy measurements
[
8
]
. This methodology has been pivotal in the analysis
[
59
]
and modeling
[
36
]
of various algorithms such as stochastic gradient descent
[
13
]
. The significance of stochastic approximation lies in its ability to handle noise and uncertainty in the data, making it a robust tool for applications in machine learning, signal processing, and econometrics, where exact function evaluations are often infeasible
[
32
,
6
]
.

One prominent application of SA is in Reinforcement Learning (RL), a domain of machine learning where agents learn to make decisions by interacting with an environment
[
80
,
81
]
. Core RL algorithms, such as Temporal Difference (TD)-learning and
Q
-learning, can be modeled and studied as special cases of SA
[
79
,
8
]
. TD-learning updates value estimates based on the difference between consecutive estimates, while Q-learning improves the action-value function estimates. These methods rely on stochastic approximation techniques to converge to optimal policies, leveraging the iterative nature of these methods to handle inherent uncertainty and variability in the learning environment
[
93
]
.

Building on the foundations of SA in RL, there has been significant interest in federated learning (FL), a paradigm that allows multiple decentralized agents to collaboratively train a model while keeping their data locally stored
[
37
]
. This approach has been extensively studied in the context of supervised learning
[
91
,
76
,
65
,
50
]
, offering solutions that preserve data privacy and reduce communication overhead
[
57
,
37
,
63
,
96
]
. Recently, some work has been done on the application of FL to RL problems (also known as FRL)
[
95
,
100
,
61
,
103
,
70
]
. Studies on federated
Q
-learning
[
94
,
26
]
, and federated TD-learning
[
86
,
73
,
90
,
89
]
investigate how decentralized agents can collectively solve RL problems without sharing their raw data, thus maintaining privacy and improving scalability
[
104
]
. These investigations represent significant progress in applying federated principles to more complex and dynamic learning environments
[
63
]
.

The goal of this paper is to develop and study federated stochastic approximation with Markovian noise and heterogeneity.
We first consider a general heterogeneous federated stochastic approximation and we establish its convergence bound. Next, we propose communication-efficient federated versions of on-policy TD, off-policy TD, and
Q
-learning algorithms. In addition, we characterize the convergence and sample complexity of these algorithms employing the convergence result of the general federated stochastic approximation.
We aim to demonstrate that our approach achieves linear speedup with respect to the number of agents involved. By addressing the challenges of heterogeneity among agents, our results can be applied to a wide range of off-policy and on-policy algorithms with function approximation. This contribution is expected to bridge the gap between stochastic approximation theory and practical federated RL applications, providing a robust framework for future research and development in the field
[
49
,
35
]
.

The main contributions and organization of the paper are summarized in the following.

•

In Section
3.2
, we propose and study a general
Fe
derated Hetero
G
eneous
S
tochastic
A
pproximation framework with
M
arkovian noise (FeGSAM). Considering Markovian sampling noise and heterogeneity poses a significant challenge in the analysis of this algorithm. The convergence result for FeGSAM serves as a workhorse that enables us to analyze both federated TD-learning and federated
Q
-learning. We characterize the convergence of FeGSAM with a refined analysis of general stochastic approximation algorithms, fundamentally improving on prior work.

•

In the on-policy setting, in Section
5
we propose and analyze federated TD-learning with linear function approximation, where the agents’ goal is to evaluate a common policy using on-policy samples collected in parallel from their environments. The agents only share the updated value function (not data) with the central server, thus saving communication cost. We prove a linear convergence speedup with the number of agents and also characterize the impact of communication frequency on the convergence.

•

In the tabular off-policy setting, in Section
6
we propose and analyze the federated off-policy TD-learning and federated
Q
-learning algorithms. Again, we establish a linear speedup in their convergence with respect to the number of agents, along with constant communication cost. Since every agent samples data using a private policy and only communicates the updated value or
Q
-function, the off-policy FRL helps to keep both the data and the policy private.

•

In the off-policy setting with function approximation, in Section
7
we propose and analyze the federated off-policy TD-learning with linear function approximation. Considering heterogeneity in the analysis of FeGSAM is essential to establish the sample complexity of this algorithm, and show a linear speedup with respect to the number of agents. However, we will observe that this setting requires more than a constant (error-dependent) number of communications.

•

We finally provide a sketch of the proof of the convergence of FeGSAM in Section
8
.

2
Related Work

In the related work section, we merge the results on stochastic approximation with their corresponding applications in RL.

Single node TD-learning and
Q
-learning.
Most existing RL literature is focused on designing and analyzing algorithms that run at a single computing node. In the on-policy setting, the asymptotic convergence of TD-learning was established in
[
85
,
82
,
11
]
, and the finite-sample bounds were studied in
[
23
,
45
,
10
,
75
,
33
,
18
]
. In the off-policy setting,
[
56
,
101
]
study the asymptotic and
[
16
,
18
]
characterize the finite time bound of TD-learning. The
Q
-learning algorithm was first proposed in
[
93
]
. There has been a long line of work to establish the convergence properties of
Q
-learning. In particular,
[
84
,
34
,
9
,
12
,
11
]
characterize the asymptotic convergence of
Q
-learning,
[
4
,
5
,
87
,
16
,
18
]
study the finite-sample convergence bound in the mean-square sense, and
[
25
,
48
,
64
]
study the high-probability convergence bounds of
Q
-learning.

Federated Learning with i.i.d. Noise.
When multiple agents are used to expedite sample collection, transferring the samples to a central server for the purpose of training can be costly in applications with high-dimensional data
[
72
]
and it may also compromise the agents’ privacy. Federated Learning is an emerging distributed optimization paradigm
[
44
,
38
]
that utilizes local computation at the agents to train models, such that only model updates, not data, is shared with the central server. In local Stochastic Gradient Descent (Local SGD or FedAvg)
[
57
,
76
]
, the core algorithm in FL, locally trained models are periodically averaged by the central server to achieve consensus among agents at a reduced communication cost. Although the convergence of local SGD has been extensively studied in prior work
[
39
,
74
,
65
,
42
]
, these works assume i.i.d. noise in the gradients, which is acceptable for SGD but too restrictive for RL algorithms.

Distributed and Multi-agent RL.
Some previous works have analyzed distributed and multi-agent RL algorithms in the presence of Markovian noise in various settings such as decentralized stochastic approximation
[
24
,
77
,
86
,
99
]
,
TD learning with linear function approximation
[
88
]
, and
off-policy TD in actor-critic algorithms
[
21
,
22
]
. However, all these works consider decentralized settings, where agents communicate with their neighbors after every local update. In
[
73
]
, a parallel implementation of the asynchronous advantage actor-critic (A3C) algorithm (which does not have local updates) has been proposed under both i.i.d. and Markov sampling. However, the authors prove a linear speedup only for the i.i.d. case, and an almost linear speedup is observed experimentally for the Markovian case.

More recently,
[
46
]
introduced an asynchronous FRL framework with policy gradient updates, offering convergence guarantees.
[
28
]
provided a global convergence guarantees for federated policy gradient methods in adversarial settings.
[
89
]
emphasized the role of momentum in collaborative FRL, addressing heterogeneity across environments. The work by
[
78
]
explored the generalization of federated learning by focusing on stability and heterogeneity’s influence.
[
26
]
and
[
94
]
investigated federated Q-learning, with
[
26
]
proposing a heterogeneous federated Q-learning framework (FedHQL) and
[
94
]
demonstrating the benefits of heterogeneity.
[
97
]
focused on federated natural policy gradient methods for multi-task reinforcement learning, while
[
35
]
addressed the challenges of environment heterogeneity in federated reinforcement learning.

3
Heterogeneous Federated Stochastic Approximation with Markovian Noise

In this section we will introduce our heterogeneous federated stochastic approximation with Markovian noise and establish the sample complexity of this algorithm. However, to lay the groundwork, we will first discuss SA in the single-node setting.

3.1
Single node

While generic stochastic approximation algorithms are studied under i.i.d. noise
[
25
,
69
,
87
,
52
,
23
]
, to apply them to studying RL we need to understand stochastic approximation under Markovian noise
[
84
,
64
,
18
,
15
]
which is significantly more challenging.

Consider a generic SA (i.i.d. or Markovian noise) with constant step size
\alpha
and parameter vector
{\mathbf{x}}_{T}
as follows

\displaystyle{\mathbf{x}}_{t+1}=(1-\alpha){\mathbf{x}}_{t}+\alpha(G({\mathbf{x}}_{t})+{\mathbf{w}}_{t}).

(1)

Here,
G(\cdot)
is the function, the fixed point of which we would like to discover, and
{\mathbf{w}}_{t}
represent the noise in observing the
G(\cdot)
function. A wide range of algorithms, from stochastic gradient descent
[
59
]
to RL
[
8
]
, can be studied as special cases of (
1
). For example, stochastic gradient descent to minimize the function
f(\cdot)
, follows the following iteration:
{\mathbf{x}}_{t+1}=(1-\alpha){\mathbf{x}}_{t}+\alpha({\mathbf{x}}_{t}-\nabla f({\mathbf{x}}_{t})+{\mathbf{w}}_{t})
, where
{\mathbf{w}}_{t}
is the noise in estimating the gradient function.

Assuming that
G(\cdot)
has
{\mathbf{x}}^{*}
as its fixed point (that is,
G({\mathbf{x}}^{*})={\mathbf{x}}^{*}
), it can be shown that the iterations in
1
have the following convergence behavior.

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\|{\mathbf{x}}_{T}-{\mathbf{x}}^{*}\|^{2}]\leq\mathcal{C}_{1}(1-\mathcal{C}_{0}\alpha)^{T}+\mathcal{C}_{2}\alpha,

(2)

where
\mathcal{C}_{0},\mathcal{C}_{1},
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
are some problem dependent positive constants (Look at Appendix
A
for a discussion on a lower bound on the convergence of general SA). The first term is denoted as the bias and the second term is called the variance. According to this bound,
{\mathbf{x}}_{T}
geometrically converges to a ball around
{\mathbf{x}}^{*}
with radius proportional to
\mathcal{C}_{2}\alpha
. Notice that we can always reduce the variance term by reducing the step size
\alpha
, but this will lead to slower convergence in the bias term. In particular, to get
{\mathbb{E}}[\|{\mathbf{x}}_{T}-{\mathbf{x}}^{*}\|^{2}]\leq\epsilon
, it is easy to see that we need
T\geq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\mathcal{C}_{2}}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)
samples. Now, suppose that the constant
\mathcal{C}_{2}
is large. In this case, the variance term in the bound in (
2
) is large, and the sample complexity, which is proportional to
\mathcal{C}_{2}
will be poor. Notice that by the discussion in Appendix
A
, this bound is tight and cannot be improved.

This is where FL can be employed to control the variance term by generating more data. An example of SA is TD-learning, which will be discussed in detail in Sections
5
,
6
, and
7
. In federated TD-learning, multiple agents work together to evaluate the value function simultaneously. Due to this collaboration, agents can estimate the true value function with a lower variance. The same holds for estimating the optimal
Q
-function in federated
Q
-learning, which will be discussed in Section
6.2
.

3.2
Generalized Heterogeneous Federated Stochastic Approximation

In this section, we study the convergence of a general heterogeneous federated stochastic approximation for contractive operators, FeGSAM, which is presented in Algorithm
1
. In this algorithm, there are
{\rm NewA}
agents
i=1,2,\dots,{\rm NewA}
, which collectively aim to find the fixed point
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
of the function
\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}G^{i}(\cdot)
that satisfies
\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}G^{i}({\bm{\theta}}^{*})={\bm{\theta}}^{*}
. In this paper, we consider heterogeneity, which means that each function
G^{i}(\cdot)
can have a different fixed point. In the special case where the fixed point of all agents coincides, we have a homogeneous federated SA, which was studied in
[
41
]
. The heterogeneity assumption is essential when studying off-policy TD-learning with linear function approximation, which will be discussed in Section
7
.

At each time step
t\geq 0
, each agent
i
maintains the parameter
{\bm{\theta}^{i}_{t}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
At time
t=0
, all agents initialize their parameters with
{\bm{\theta}}_{0}^{i}={\bm{\theta}}_{0}
. Next, at time
t\geq 0
, each agent
i
updates its parameter to
{\bm{\theta}}^{i}_{t+1}={\bm{\theta}^{i}_{t}}+\alpha\left({\mathbf{G}^{i}}({\bm{\theta}^{i}_{t}},{\mathbf{y}^{i}_{t}})-{\bm{\theta}^{i}_{t}}+\mathbf{b}^{i}({\mathbf{y}^{i}_{t}})\right)
. Here,
\alpha
denotes the step size and
{\mathbf{y}^{i}_{t}}
is a Markovian noise throughout time
t
, but independent of all agents
j\neq i
. This notion is defined more concretely in Assumption
3.4
.
We note that functions
{\mathbf{G}^{i}}(\cdot,\cdot)
and
\mathbf{b}^{i}(\cdot)
are allowed to be dependent on the agent
i
. This allows us to employ the convergence bound of FeGSAM in order to derive the convergence bound of off-policy TD-learning with different behavior policies across agents. To avoid divergence, for every
K
time step, we synchronize the parameters of all agents as
{\bm{\theta}^{i}_{t}}\leftarrow{\bm{\theta}_{t}}\triangleq\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{j=1}^{\rm NewA}{\bm{\theta}}_{t}^{j},
for all
i\in[{\rm NewA}]
. Note that although smaller
K
corresponds to more frequent synchronization and hence more “accurate” updates, at the same time it results in a higher communication cost, which is not desirable.
Hence, to determine the optimal choice of the synchronization period, it is essential to characterize the dependence of the convergence on
K
. This is one of the results we will derive in Theorem
B.1
. Finally, the algorithm samples
\hat{T}\sim q_{T}^{c}
, where
q^{c}_{T}(t)=\frac{c^{-t}}{\sum_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T-1}c^{-t^{\prime}}}
and outputs
{\bm{\theta}}_{\hat{T}}
. This sampling scheme is essential for the convergence of the overall algorithm. We also make some assumptions regarding the underlying process.

Algorithm 1

Federated Heterogeneous Stochastic Approximation with Markovian Noise (FeGSAM)

1:

Input:

c_{FSAM},T,{\bm{\theta}}_{0},K,\alpha

2:

{\bm{\theta}}^{i}_{0}={\bm{\theta}}_{0}
for all
i=1,\dots,{\rm NewA}
.

3:

for

t=0
to
T-1

do

4:

{\bm{\theta}}^{i}_{t+1}={\bm{\theta}^{i}_{t}}+\alpha\left({\mathbf{G}^{i}}({\bm{\theta}^{i}_{t}},{\mathbf{y}^{i}_{t}})-{\bm{\theta}^{i}_{t}}+\mathbf{b}^{i}({\mathbf{y}^{i}_{t}})\right),\forall\ i\in[{\rm NewA}]

5:

if

t+1
mod
K=0

then

6:

{\bm{\theta}}^{i}_{t+1}={\bm{\theta}}_{t+1}\triangleq\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{j=1}^{\rm NewA}{\bm{\theta}}^{j}_{t+1},\forall\ i\in[{\rm NewA}]

7:

end

if

8:

end

for

9:

Sample
\hat{T}\sim q_{T}^{c_{FSAM}}(\cdot)
.

10:

Return:

\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}{\bm{\theta}}^{i}_{\hat{T}}

First, we consider assumption
3.1
where we assume that the expectation of
{\mathbf{G}^{i}}({\bm{\theta}},{\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{i})
geometrically converges to some function
{\bar{\mathbf{G}}}^{i}({\bm{\theta}})
and the expectation of
\mathbf{b}^{i}({\mathbf{y}}^{i}_{t})
converges to
\bar{\mathbf{b}}^{i}
as
t\rightarrow\infty
, and the fixed point of
{\bar{\mathbf{G}}}^{i}(\cdot)+\bar{\mathbf{b}}^{i}
is denoted as
{\bm{\theta}}^{i,*}
satisfies
{\bar{\mathbf{G}}}^{i}({\bm{\theta}}^{i,*})+\bar{\mathbf{b}}^{i}={\bm{\theta}}^{i,*}
. Note that the fixed points
{\bm{\theta}}^{i,*}
can vary by agent
i
. This is the source of heterogeneity in our model. In addition, the expectation of
\mathbf{b}^{i}({\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{i})
is bounded by a geometrically converging term plus
m_{3}\eta_{i}
, where
\eta_{i}=\|{\bm{\theta}}^{i,*}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\|_{c}
, and
\|\cdot\|_{c}
is a fixed norm. Note that in the homogeneous setting studied in
[
41
]
, we have
\eta_{i}=0
for all
i
.

Assumption 3.1
.

For every agent
i
, there exist a function
{\bar{\mathbf{G}}}^{i}({\bm{\theta}})
such that we have

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{G}^{i}}({\bm{\theta}},{\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{i})]

\displaystyle={\bar{\mathbf{G}}}^{i}({\bm{\theta}}).

Furthermore, there exists
m_{1},m_{2},m_{3},m_{4}\geq 0
and
\rho\in[0,1)
, such that for every
i=1,2,\dots,{\rm NewA}
,

\displaystyle\|{\bar{\mathbf{G}}}^{i}({\bm{\theta}})-{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{G}^{i}}({\bm{\theta}},{\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{i})]\|_{c}

\displaystyle\leq m_{1}\|{\bm{\theta}}\|_{c}\rho^{t}

(3)

\displaystyle\|{\mathbb{E}}[\mathbf{b}^{i}({\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{i})]\|_{c}

\displaystyle\leq m_{2}\rho^{t}+m_{3}\eta_{i}

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}\mathbf{b}^{i}({\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{i})\right]\right\|_{c}

\displaystyle\leq m_{4}\rho^{t},

where
\|\cdot\|_{c}
is a given norm.

We further denote
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}\eta_{i}
which characterizes the level of heterogeneity in the system.

Next, we assume a contraction property on the expected operator
{\bar{\mathbf{G}}}^{i}({\bm{\theta}})
.

Assumption 3.2
.

We assume all expected operators
{\bar{\mathbf{G}}}^{i}({\bm{\theta}})
are contraction mappings with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{c}
norm with contraction factor
\gamma_{c}\in(0,1)
. That is, for all
i=1,2,\dots,{\rm NewA}
,

\displaystyle\|{\bar{\mathbf{G}}}^{i}({\bm{\theta}}_{1})-{\bar{\mathbf{G}}}^{i}({\bm{\theta}}_{2})\|_{c}\leq\gamma_{c}\|{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-{\bm{\theta}}_{2}\|_{c},\quad\text{for all }{\bm{\theta}}_{1},{\bm{\theta}}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Next, we consider some Lipschitz and boundedness properties on
{\mathbf{G}^{i}}(\cdot,\cdot)
and
\mathbf{b}^{i}(\cdot)
.

Assumption 3.3
.

For all
i=1,\dots,{\rm NewA}
, there exist constants
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
and
B
such that

1.

\|{\mathbf{G}^{i}}({\bm{\theta}}_{1},{\mathbf{y}})-{\mathbf{G}^{i}}({\bm{\theta}}_{2},{\mathbf{y}})\|_{c}\leq A_{1}\|{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-{\bm{\theta}}_{2}\|_{c}
for all
{\bm{\theta}}_{1},{\bm{\theta}}_{2},{\mathbf{y}}
.

2.

\|{\mathbf{G}^{i}}({\bm{\theta}},{\mathbf{y}})\|_{c}\leq A_{2}\|{\bm{\theta}}\|_{c}
for all
{\bm{\theta}},{\mathbf{y}}
.

3.

\|\mathbf{b}^{i}({\mathbf{y}}^{i})\|_{c}\leq B
for all
{\mathbf{y}^{i}}
.

Finally, we impose an assumption on the random noise
{\mathbf{y}^{i}_{t}}
.

Assumption 3.4
.

We assume that the Markovian noise
{\mathbf{y}^{i}_{t}}
(Markovian with respect to time
t
) is independent across agents
i
. In other words, for all measurable functions
f(\cdot)
and
g(\cdot)
, we assume the following

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{t-r}[f({\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{i})\times g({\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{j})]={\mathbb{E}}_{t-r}[f({\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{i})]\times{\mathbb{E}}_{t-r}[g({\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{j})]\quad\text{for all}\quad r\leq t,i\neq j.

3.3
Main result

Theorem
3.1
states the convergence of Algorithm
1
, which is the main result of this paper.

Theorem 3.1
.

Consider the federated heterogeneous stochastic approximation Algorithm
1
with
c_{FSAM}=1-\frac{\alpha{\varphi}_{2}}{2}\in(0,1)
(
{\varphi}_{2}
is defined in Equation (
28
) in the appendix), and synchronization frequency
K
. Denote
{\bm{\theta}_{t}}=\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}{\bm{\theta}^{i}_{t}}
, and consider
{\bm{\theta}}_{\hat{T}}
as the output of this algorithm after
T
iterations. Assume
\tau_{\alpha}=\lceil 2\log_{\rho}\frac{1}{\alpha}\rceil
. For
T\geq\max\{K+\tau_{\alpha},2\tau_{\alpha}\}
and small enough step size
\alpha
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\|{\bm{\theta}}_{\hat{T}}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\|^{2}_{c}]\leq\mathcal{C}_{1}\frac{1}{\alpha}c_{FSAM}^{T-2\tau_{\alpha}+1}+\mathcal{C}_{2}\frac{\alpha\tau_{\alpha}^{2}}{{\rm NewA}}+\mathcal{C}_{3}(K-1)\alpha^{2}\tau_{\alpha}+\mathcal{C}_{4}(K-1)^{2}\varepsilon\alpha^{2}\tau_{\alpha}+\mathcal{C}_{5}\alpha^{3}\tau_{\alpha}^{2},

(4)

where

\mathcal{C}_{i}
,
i=1,2,3,4,5
are some constants which are specified precisely in Appendix
B
, and are independent of
K,\alpha,{\rm NewA}
.

Theorem
3.1
establishes the convergence of
{\bm{\theta}}_{\hat{T}}
to
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
in the expected mean-squared sense. The first term in (
4
) converges geometrically to zero as
T
grows. The second term is proportional to
\alpha
similar to (
2
). However, the number of agents
{\rm NewA}
in the denominator ensures
linear speedup
, meaning that for small enough
\alpha
(such that
\alpha/{\rm NewA}
is the dominant term), the sample complexity of each individual agent, relative to a centralized system, is reduced by a factor of
{\rm NewA}
. The third term has quadratic dependence on
\alpha
, and is zero when we have perfect synchronization, that is,
K=1
. The fourth term is quadratic in
K-1
and the step size
\alpha
. This term vanishes when we have complete homogeneity among the agents.

The last term is proportional to
\alpha^{3}
, and has the weakest dependency on the step size
\alpha
. For
K>1
, we can merge this term with the third and fourth terms by the upper bound
\alpha^{3}\leq\alpha^{2}
. The current upper bound, however, is tighter since with
K=1
(i.e. perfect synchronization) we have no term of the order
\alpha^{2}
. Note that similar bounds have been established for the simpler federated setting with i.i.d. noise
[
39
,
42
]
and in the Markovian noise
[
41
]
. Here we achieved this result for the more general heterogeneous federated setting with Markov noise.

Remark
.

The bound in Theorem
3.1
is valid only after
T>\max\{K+\tau_{\alpha},2\tau_{\alpha}\}
and for all synchronization periods
K\geq 1
. At
K=1
the third and fourth terms in the bound disappear, and we will be left only with the first-order term, which is linearly decreasing with respect to the number of agents
{\rm NewA}
, and the third-order term
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{3})
. The last term, however, is not tight and can be further improved to be of the order
\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{j}),j>3
. But for that, we need to assume a larger
\tau_{\alpha}
, which means that the bound only holds after a longer waiting time. In particular, by choosing
\tau_{\alpha}=\lceil r\log_{\rho}\alpha\rceil
, we can get
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{2r-1})
for the last term (see the proof of Lemma
B.2
).

Next, in Corollary
3.1.1
we establish the sample complexity and the communication complexity of Algorithm
1
in homogeneous and heterogeneous settings.

Corollary 3.1.1
.

1.

In the homogeneous setting, where
\varepsilon=0
, choosing
\alpha=\frac{8\log({\rm NewA}T)}{T{\varphi}_{2}}
and
K=T/{\rm NewA}
, we get
T=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}\epsilon}\right)
sample complexity for achieving
{\mathbb{E}}[\|{\bm{\theta}}_{\hat{T}}\|^{2}_{c}]\leq\epsilon
. Furthermore, we get
T/K=N
communication complexity, which is independent of the error
\epsilon
.

2.

In the heterogeneous setting, where
\varepsilon>0
, choosing
\alpha=\frac{8\log({\rm NewA}T)}{T{\varphi}_{2}}
and
K=\sqrt{T}/{\rm NewA}
, we get
T=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}\epsilon}\right)
sample complexity for achieving
{\mathbb{E}}[\|{\bm{\theta}}_{\hat{T}}\|^{2}_{c}]\leq\epsilon
. Furthermore, we get
T/K=\sqrt{N}/\sqrt{\epsilon}
communication complexity. The communication complexity in this case is dependent on the error
\epsilon
.

The core idea that allows us to obtain a tight bound is as follows. Suppose the random variable
X
has bounded variance
\sigma^{2}
, and suppose
X_{i},i=1,\dots,{\rm NewA}
are i.i.d. samples of this random variable. It is easy to show that the variance of
\bar{X}=\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}X_{i}/{\rm NewA}
is
\sigma^{2}/{\rm NewA}
, and this is how we can attain the linear speedup in the i.i.d. and homogeneous setting. However, with Markovian noise and heterogeneity among the agents, the problem is significantly more challenging, and the existing work fails to achieve linear speedup. In the homogeneous setting, where
\varepsilon=0
,
[
41
]
has established the sample and communication complexity of Corollary
3.1.1
. However, the more general heterogeneous setting requires a more refined analysis (see Proposition
B.4
).

4
Single agent Reinforcement Learning

Next, we discuss and explain the underlying setting of RL. We model our RL setting with an MDP with 5 tuples
(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{R},\gamma)
, where
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{A}
are finite sets of states and actions,
\mathcal{P}
is the set of transition probabilities,
\mathcal{R}
is the reward function and
\gamma\in(0,1)
denotes the discount factor. At each time step
t
, the system is in some state
S_{t}
, and the agent takes some action
A_{t}\sim\pi(\cdot|S_{t})
, where
\pi
is the policy at hand. Given the state
S_{t}
and the chosen action
A_{t}
, the agent receives the reward
\mathcal{R}(S_{t},A_{t})
. In the next time step, the system transitions to a new state
S_{t+1}
according to the state transition probability
\mathcal{P}(\cdot|S_{t},A_{t})
. This series of states and actions
(S_{t},A_{t})_{t\geq 0}
constructs a Markov chain, which is the source of Markovian noise in RL. Throughout this paper, we assume that this Markov chain is irreducible and aperiodic (also known as ergodic). It is known that this Markov chain asymptotically converges to a steady state, and we denote its stationary distribution by
\mu^{\pi}
.

To measure the long-term reward achieved by following policy
\pi
, we define the value function

\displaystyle V^{\pi}(s)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\mathcal{R}(S_{t},A_{t})|S_{0}=s,A_{t}\sim\pi(\cdot|S_{t})\right].

(5)

Equation (
5
) is the tabular representation of the value function. However, sometimes the size of the state space
|\mathcal{S}|
is large and storing
V^{\pi}(s)
for all
s\in\mathcal{S}
is computationally infeasible. Hence, a low-dimensional vector
{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
d\ll|\mathcal{S}|
, can be used to approximate the value function as
V^{\pi}(s)\approx\phi(s)^{\top}{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}

[
85
]
. Here,
\phi(s)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a given feature vector corresponding to the state
s
. Using a low-dimensional vector
{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}
to approximate a high-dimensional vector
(V^{\pi}(s))_{s\in\mathcal{S}}
is referred to as the function approximation paradigm in RL. The task of evaluating this value function is denoted as the Temporal Difference (TD)-Learning. For each pair
(s,a)
, we also define the
Q
-function,
Q^{\pi}(s,a)={\mathbb{E}}[\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\mathcal{R}(S_{t},A_{t})|S_{0}=s,A_{0}=a,A_{t}\sim\pi(\cdot|S_{t})]
, which will be used in
Q
-learning. Both TD-learning and
Q
-learning can be seen as variants of stochastic approximation
[
84
,
17
,
19
,
20
,
15
]
. In sections
4.1
and
4.2
we will discuss TD-learning and
Q
-learning in more detail.

4.1
Temporal Difference Learning

An intermediate goal in RL is to estimate the value function (either
(V^{\pi}(s))_{s\in\mathcal{S}}
or
{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}
) corresponding to a particular policy
\pi
using data collected from the environment.
This task is denoted as
policy evaluation
and one of the commonly used approaches to accomplish this is TD-learning
[
79
]
. TD-learning is an iterative algorithm where the elements of a
d
(or
|\mathcal{S}|
, in the tabular setting) dimensional vector is updated until it converges to
{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}
(or
V^{\pi}
). This evaluated value function can be employed in different RL algorithms such as actor-critic
[
43
]
.

In online TD-learning with linear function approximation, we consider a full rank feature matrix
\Phi\in|\mathcal{S}|\times d
, and we denote the
s
-th row of this matrix with
\phi(s),s\in\mathcal{S}
. The goal is to find
{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
which solves the following fixed point equation:

\displaystyle\Phi{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}=\Pi_{\Phi}((\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{n}\Phi{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}).

(6)

In equation (
6
),
\Pi_{\Phi}(\cdot)
is the projection with respect to the weighted 2-norm, that is,
\Pi_{\Phi}({\mathbf{V}})=\argmin_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\Phi{\mathbf{v}}-{\mathbf{V}}\|_{\pi}
. Here,
\|{\mathbf{V}}\|_{\pi}=\sqrt{{\mathbf{V}}^{\top}{\bm{\mu}}^{\pi}{\mathbf{V}}}
and
{\bm{\mu}}^{\pi}
is a diagonal matrix with diagonal entries corresponding to
\mu^{\pi}
. In equation (
6
),
(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{n}
denotes the
n
-step Bellman operator
[
85
]
. It is known
[
85
]
that equation (
6
) has a unique solution
{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}
, and
(\Phi{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}(s))_{(s\in\mathcal{S})}
is “close” to the true value function
(V^{\pi}(s))_{s\in\mathcal{S}}
. The update of the
n
-step TD-learning is as follows

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{Sample}&\quad A_{t+n}\sim\pi(\cdot|S_{t+n}),S_{t+n+1}\sim{\mathcal{P}}(\cdot|S_{t+n},A_{t+n})\\
\text{Update}&\quad{\mathbf{v}}_{t+1}={\mathbf{v}_{t}}+\alpha\phi(S_{t})\sum_{l=t}^{t+n-1}\gamma^{l-t}(\mathcal{R}(S_{l},A_{l})+\!\gamma\phi(S_{l\!+\!1})^{\top}{\mathbf{v}_{t}}\!-\!\phi(S_{l})^{\top}{\mathbf{v}_{t}}),\end{split}

(7)

where
\alpha
is the step size. The
n
-step TD-learning is an iterative algorithm to obtain
{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}
using samples from the environment. Note that in this algorithm, states and actions are sampled over a single trajectory, and hence the noise in updating
{\mathbf{v}}_{t}
is Markovian. Furthermore, since the policy which samples the actions and the evaluating policy are both
\pi
, this algorithm is on-policy. As described in
[
85
,
8
]
, the TD-learning algorithm can be studied under the umbrella of linear stochastic approximation with Markovian noise. More recently, the authors of
[
10
,
75
]
have shown that the update parameter of TD-learning
{\mathbf{v}}_{t}
converges to
{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}
in the form
{\mathbb{E}}[\|{\mathbf{v}}_{t}-{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}\|^{2}_{2}]\leq\mathcal{O}((1-\mathcal{C}_{0}\alpha)^{t}+\alpha)
. In Section
5
, we show how FRL can improve this result.

As mentioned earlier, in this section, we studied the on-policy setting, where the evaluating policy and the sampling policy coincide. In contrast, in the off-policy setting, these two policies can in general differ, and we need to account for this difference while running the algorithm. We will further expand on off-policy TD-learning and its variants in Sections
6
and
7
.

4.2
Control Problem and
Q
-learning

Assuming some initial distribution
\xi
on the state space, the average value function corresponding to policy
\pi
is defined as
V^{\pi}(\xi)={\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\xi}[V^{\pi}(s)]
.
This scalar quantity is a metric of the average long-term rewards achieved by the agent, when it starts from distribution
\xi
and follows policy
\pi
. The ultimate goal of the agent is to obtain an optimal policy
\pi^{*}
that results in the maximum long-term rewards, i.e.
\pi^{*}\in\argmax_{\pi}V^{\pi}(\xi)
.
Throughout the paper, we denote the parameters corresponding to the optimal policy with
∗
, e.g.,
V^{\pi^{*}}(\xi)\equiv V^{*}(\xi)
. The task of obtaining the optimal policy in RL is denoted as the
control problem
.

Q
-learning
[
93
,
84
]
is one of the most widely used algorithms in RL to solve the control problem. The goal of
Q
-learning is to evaluate
Q^{*}
. Knowing
Q^{*}
, one can obtain an optimal policy through greedy selection
[
62
]
, and hence resolve the control problem. Suppose
\{S_{t},A_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is generated by a fixed behavior policy
\pi_{b}
. At each time step
t
,
Q
-learning preserves a
|\mathcal{S}|.|\mathcal{A}|
dimensional table
Q_{t}
and updates the elements of this table as

\displaystyle\begin{split}{Q}_{t+1}(s,a)=\begin{cases}{Q}_{t}(S_{t},A_{t})+\alpha\left(\mathcal{R}(S_{t},A_{t})+\gamma\max_{a}{Q}_{t}(S_{t+1},a)-{Q}_{t}(S_{t},A_{t})\right)&\text{ if }(s,a)=(S_{t},A_{t})\\
{Q}_{t}(s,a)&\text{ o.w.}\end{cases}\end{split}

(8)

Taking
\pi_{b}
as an ergodic policy, the asymptotic convergence of
Q_{t}
to
Q^{*}
has been established in
[
9
]
. Furthermore, it can be shown that
Q
-learning is a special case of stochastic approximation and enjoys a convergence bound similar to (
2
)
[
3
,
48
,
64
,
18
]
.

Two points worth mentioning about the
Q
-learning algorithm. Firstly,
Q
-learning is an off-policy algorithm in the sense that only samples from a fixed ergodic policy are needed to perform the algorithm. Secondly, unlike TD-learning, the update of
Q
-learning is
non-online
. This imposes a sharp contrast between the analysis of
Q
-learning and TD-learning
[
20
]
.

5
On-policy Federated Reinforcement Learning

The federated version of the on-policy
n
-step TD-learning with linear function approximation is shown in Algorithm
2
. In this algorithm, we consider
{\rm NewA}
agents that collaborate to evaluate
{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}
. For each agent
i,i=1,2,\dots,{\rm NewA}
, we initialize their corresponding parameters
{\mathbf{v}}^{i}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
. Furthermore, each agent
i
samples its initial state
S_{0}^{i}
from some given distribution
\xi
. In the next time steps, each agent follows a single Markovian trajectory generated by policy
\pi
, independently of other agents. At each time
t
, the parameter of each agent
i
is updated using this independently generated trajectory as
{\mathbf{v}}_{t+1}^{i}={\mathbf{v}^{i}_{t}}+\alpha\phi(S_{t}^{i})E_{t,n}^{i}
. Finally, in order to ensure convergence to a global optimum, in every
K
time step all agents send their parameters to a central server. The central server evaluates the average of these parameters and returns this average to each of the agents. Each agent then continues their update procedure using this average.

Note that the averaging step is essential to ensure synchronization among agents. Smaller
K
results in more frequent synchronization, and hence better convergence guarantees. However, setting a smaller
K
is equivalent to more number of communications between individual agents and the central server, which incurs higher cost. Hence, an intermediate value for
K
must be chosen to strike a balance between the cost of communication and the accuracy. At the end, the algorithm samples a time step
\hat{T}\sim q^{c}_{T}
, where

\displaystyle q^{c}_{T}(t)=\frac{c^{-t}}{\sum_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T-1}c^{-t^{\prime}}}\quad\text{for}\quad t=0,1,\dots,T-1

(9)

and
c>1
is some constant. Since we have
q^{c}_{T}(t)\geq 0
and
\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}q^{c}_{T}(t)=1
, it is clear that
q^{c}_{T}(\cdot)
is a probability distribution over the time interval
[0,T-1]
. In Theorem
5.1
we characterize the convergence of this algorithm as a function of
\alpha
,
{\rm NewA}
, and
K
. Throughout the paper,
\tilde{O}(\cdot)
ignores the logarithmic terms.

Algorithm 2

Federated
n
-step TD (On-policy, Function Approximation)

1:

Input:
Policy
\pi,\xi

2:

Initialization:

{\mathbf{v}}^{i}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
and
S_{0}^{i}\sim\xi
and
\{A_{l}^{i},S_{l+1}^{i}\}\sim\pi
for
0\leq l\leq n-1
and all
i

3:

for

t=0
to
T-1

do

4:

for

i=1,\dots,{\rm NewA}

do

5:

Sample
A_{t+n}^{i}\sim\pi(\cdot|S_{t+n}^{i}),S_{t+n+1}^{i}\sim{\mathcal{P}}(\cdot|S_{t+n}^{i},A_{t+n}^{i})

6:

e_{t,l}^{i}\!=\!\mathcal{R}(S_{l}^{i},A_{l}^{i})\!+\!\gamma\phi(S_{l\!+\!1}^{i})^{\top}{\mathbf{v}^{i}_{t}}\!-\!\phi(S_{l}^{i})^{\top}{\mathbf{v}^{i}_{t}}
for
l=t,\dots,t+n-1

7:

E_{t,n}^{i}=\sum_{l=t}^{t+n-1}\gamma^{l-t}e_{t,l}^{i}

8:

{\mathbf{v}}_{t+1}^{i}={\mathbf{v}^{i}_{t}}+\alpha\phi(S_{t}^{i})E_{t,n}^{i}

9:

end

for

10:

if

t+1
mod
K=0

then

11:

{\mathbf{v}}^{i}_{t+1}\leftarrow\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{j=1}^{\rm NewA}{\mathbf{v}}^{j}_{t+1},\forall\ i\in[{\rm NewA}]

12:

end

if

13:

end

for

14:

Sample
\hat{T}\sim q^{c_{TDL}}_{T}

15:

Return:

\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}{\mathbf{v}}^{i}_{\hat{T}}

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
{\mathbf{v}}_{\hat{T}}=\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}{\mathbf{v}}^{i}_{\hat{T}}
denote the average of the parameters between agents at random time
\hat{T}
. For small enough step size
\alpha
, and
T\geq\mathcal{O}(\log\frac{1}{\alpha})
, there exist constant
c_{TDL}\in(0,1)
(see Section
C.1
for a precise statement), such that we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\|{\mathbf{v}}_{\hat{T}}-{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}\|_{2}^{2}]\leq\mathcal{C}_{1}^{TD_{L}}\frac{1}{\alpha}(1-\alpha\mathcal{C}_{0}^{TD_{L}})^{T}+\mathcal{C}_{2}^{TD_{L}}\frac{\alpha\tau_{\alpha}^{2}}{{\rm NewA}}+\mathcal{C}_{3}^{TD_{L}}(K-1)\alpha^{2}\tau_{\alpha}+\mathcal{C}_{4}^{TD_{L}}\alpha^{3}\tau_{\alpha}^{2},

where
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{TD_{L}}
,
i=0,1,2,3,4
are problem dependent constants, and
\tau_{\alpha}=\mathcal{O}(\log(1/\alpha))
. Choosing
\alpha=\mathcal{O}(\frac{\log({\rm NewA}T)}{T})
and
K=T/{\rm NewA}
, we achieve
{\mathbb{E}}[\|{\mathbf{v}}_{\hat{T}}-{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}\|_{2}^{2}]\leq\epsilon
within
T=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}\epsilon}\right)
iterations.

For brevity purposes, here we did not show the exact dependence of the constants
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{TD_{L}}
,
i=0,1,2,3
on the problem-dependent constants. For a discussion of the detailed expression, see Section
C.1
in the appendix.

Theorem
5.1
shows that federated TD-learning with linear function approximation enjoys a linear speedup with respect to the number of agents. Compared to the convergence bound of general stochastic approximation in (
2
), the bound in Theorem
5.1
has three differences. Firstly, the variance term that is proportional to the step size
\alpha
is divided by the number of agents
{\rm NewA}
. This will allow us to control the variance (and hence improve sample complexity) by employing more number of agents. Secondly, we have an extra term that is zero with perfect synchronization
K=1
. Although this term is not divided with
{\rm NewA}
, it is proportional to
\alpha^{2}
, which is one order higher than the variance term in (
2
). Finally, the last term is of the order
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{3})
, which can be handled by choosing a small enough step size.

Furthermore, according to the choice of
K
in Theorem
5.1
, after
T
iterations, the communication cost of federated TD is
T/K={{\rm NewA}}
. However, by employing federated TD-learning in the naive setting where all agents communicate with the central server at every time step, the communication cost will be
\mathcal{O}(T)
. Hence, we observe that by carefully tuning the hyper parameters of federated TD-learning, we can significantly reduce the communication cost of the overall algorithm, while not losing performance in terms of the sample complexity.

Finally, the federated TD-learning Algorithm
2
preserves the privacy of agents. In particular, since single agents only need to share their parameters
{\mathbf{v}}_{t+1}^{i}
, the central server will not be exposed to the state-action-reward trajectory generated by each agent. This can be essential in some applications where privacy is an issue
[
58
,
83
]
.
Examples of such applications include autonomous driving
[
51
,
102
]
,
Internet of Things (IoT)
[
60
,
67
,
92
]
, and cloud robotics
[
53
,
95
]
.

Remark
.

In algorithm
2
, the randomness in choosing
\hat{T}
is independent of all the other randomness in the problem. Hence, in a practical setting, one can sample
\hat{T}
in advance, before running the algorithm, and stop the algorithm at time step
\hat{T}
and output
{\mathbf{v}}_{\hat{T}}
. By this method, we require only one data point to be saved, which results in the memory complexity of
\mathcal{O}(1)
for the algorithm.

6
Off-Policy Federated Reinforcement Learning with Tabular Representation

On-policy TD-learning requires online sampling from the environment, which might be costly (e.g. in robotics
[
31
,
47
]
), high risk (e.g., in self-driving cars
[
98
,
55
]
), or unethical (e.g., in clinical trials
[
30
,
54
,
29
]
).
Off-policy training in RL refers to the paradigm in which we use data collected by a fixed behavior policy to run the algorithm. When used in a federated setting, off-policy RL also has privacy advantages
[
27
,
63
,
104
]
. In particular, suppose that each single agent attains a unique sampling policy and does not wish to reveal these policies to the central server. In off-policy FL, agents only transmit sampled data, and hence the sampling policies remain private to each agent.

In Section
6.1
, we discuss off-policy TD learning, and in Section
6.2
, we cover
Q
-learning, an off-policy control algorithm. Additionally, we examine off-policy TD learning with linear function approximation in Section
7
. It is important to note that federated off-policy TD learning with function approximation leads to heterogeneous federated learning, which presents additional challenges. Therefore, we dedicate a separate section to address these complexities.”

6.1
Federated Off-Policy TD-learning

In the following, we first discuss single-node off-policy TD-learning, and then we generalize it to the federated setting.

6.1.1
Off-policy TD-learning

In off-policy TD-learning the goal is to evaluate the value function
{\mathbf{V}}^{\pi}=(V^{\pi}(s))_{s\in\mathcal{S}}
corresponding to the policy
\pi
using data sampled from some fixed behavior policy
\pi_{b}
. In this setting, the evaluating policy
\pi
and the sampling policy
\pi_{b}
can be arbitrarily different, and we must account for this difference while performing the evaluation. Although
\pi
and
\pi_{b}
can be different, notice that the value function
{\mathbf{V}}^{\pi}
does not depend on
\pi_{b}
. In order to account for this difference, we introduce the notion of
importance sampling
as
\mathfrak{I}^{b}(s,a)=\frac{\pi(a|s)}{\pi^{b}(a|s)}
which is employed in the off-policy TD-learning.

Several works studied the finite-time convergence of off-policy TD-learning. In particular, the authors in
[
40
,
18
,
17
,
19
]
show that, similar to on-policy TD, off-policy TD-learning can be studied under the umbrella of stochastic approximation. Hence, this algorithm has a similar convergence behavior as (
2
).

6.1.2
Federated off-policy tabular TD-learning

The federated version of
n
-step off-policy tabular TD-learning is shown in Algorithm
3
. In this algorithm, each agent
i
attains a unique (and private) sampling policy
\pi^{i}
and follows an independent trajectory generated by this policy. Furthermore, at each time step
t
, each agent
i
attains a
|\mathcal{S}|
-dimensional vector
{\mathbf{V}}^{i}_{t}
and updates this vector using the samples generated by
\pi^{i}
. In order to account for off-policy sampling, each agent utilizes
\mathfrak{I}^{(i)}(S^{i},A^{i})=\frac{\pi(A^{i}|S^{i})}{\pi^{i}(A^{i}|S^{i})}
in the update of their algorithm. We further define
\mathfrak{I}_{\max}=\max_{s,a,i}\mathfrak{I}^{(i)}(s,a)
, which is a measure of the discrepancy between the evaluation policy
\pi
and the sampling policy
\pi^{i}
of all agents.

To ensure synchronization, all agents transmit their parameter vectors to the central server at every
K
time step. The central server returns the average of these vectors to each agent, and each agent follows this averaged vector afterwards. Notice that in federated off-policy TD-learning Algorithm
3
, each agent shares neither their sampled trajectory of state-action-rewards, nor their sampling policy with the central server. This provides two levels of privacy for the single agents. In the end, the algorithm samples a time step
\hat{T}\sim q_{T}^{c_{TD}}
, where the distribution
q_{T}^{c}
is defined in (
9
) and
c_{TD}=1-\frac{\alpha{\varphi}_{TD}}{2}
, where
{\varphi}_{TD}=1-\frac{0.5e^{1/4}(2-\mu_{\min}(1-\gamma^{n+1}))}{\sqrt{\sqrt{e}-1+\left(\frac{2-\mu_{\min}(1-\gamma^{n+1})}{2-2\mu_{\min}(1-\gamma^{n+1})}\right)^{2}}}
. Here, we denote
\mu_{\min}=\min_{s,i}\mu^{\pi^{i}}(s)
. The constant
c_{TD}
is carefully chosen to ensure the convergence of Algorithm
3
. Furthermore, for a step size small enough
\alpha
, it can be shown that
0<c_{TD}<1
.

Algorithm 3

Federated
n
-step TD (Off-policy Tabular Setting)

1:

Input:
Policy
\pi,\xi

2:

Initialization:

{\mathbf{V}}_{0}^{i}=\mathbf{0}
and
S_{0}^{i}\sim\xi
and
\{A_{l}^{i},S_{l+1}^{i}\}\sim\pi^{i}
for
0\leq l\leq n-1
and all
i

3:

for

t=0
to
T-1

do

4:

for

i=1,\dots,{\rm NewA}

do

5:

Sample
A_{t+n}^{i}\sim\pi^{i}(\cdot|S_{t+n}^{i}),S_{t+n+1}^{i}\sim{\mathcal{P}}(\cdot|S_{t+n}^{i},A_{t+n}^{i})

6:

e_{t,l}^{i}=\mathcal{R}(S_{l}^{i},A_{l}^{i})\!+\!\gamma{\mathbf{V}}_{t}^{i}(S_{l+1}^{i})-{\mathbf{V}}_{t}^{i}(S_{l}^{i})

7:

{\mathbf{V}}_{t+1}^{i}(s)=\begin{cases}{\mathbf{V}}_{t}^{i}(s)+\alpha\sum_{l=t}^{t+n-1}\gamma^{l-t}\left[\Pi_{j=t}^{l}\mathfrak{I}^{(i)}(S^{i}_{j},A^{i}_{j})\right]e_{t,l}^{i}&\text{ if }s=S_{t}^{i}\\
{\mathbf{V}}_{t}^{i}(s)&\text{ o.w }\end{cases}

8:

end

for

9:

if

t+1
mod
K=0

then

10:

{\mathbf{V}}_{t+1}^{i}\leftarrow\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{j=1}^{\rm NewA}{\mathbf{V}}^{j}_{t+1},\forall\ i\in[{\rm NewA}]

11:

end

if

12:

end

for

13:

Sample

\hat{T}\sim q_{T}^{c_{TD}}

14:

Return:
\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}{\mathbf{V}}^{i}_{\hat{T}}

Theorem
6.1
states the convergence of this Algorithm.

Theorem 6.1
.

Consider the federated
n
-step off-policy tabular TD-learning Algorithm
3
. Denote
{\mathbf{V}}_{\hat{T}}=\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}{\mathbf{V}}_{\hat{T}}^{i}
. For small enough step size
\alpha
and large enough
T
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\|{\mathbf{V}}_{\hat{T}}-{\mathbf{V}}^{\pi}\|_{\infty}^{2}]\leq\mathcal{C}_{1}^{TD_{T}}\frac{1}{\alpha}c_{TD}^{T}+\mathcal{C}_{2}^{TD_{T}}\frac{\alpha\tau_{\alpha}^{2}}{{\rm NewA}}+\mathcal{C}_{3}^{TD_{T}}(K-1)\alpha^{2}\tau_{\alpha},

where
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{TD_{T}}=\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{1}^{TD_{T}}.\frac{\mathfrak{I}_{\max}^{4n-2}}{\mu_{\min}(1-\gamma)^{3}}
,
\mathcal{C}_{2}^{TD_{T}}=\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{2}^{TD_{T}}.\frac{\mathfrak{I}_{\max}^{3n-1}|\mathcal{S}|\log^{2}(|\mathcal{S}|)}{\mu_{\min}^{2}(1-\gamma)^{4}}
,
\mathcal{C}_{3}^{TD_{T}}=\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{3}^{TD_{T}}.\frac{\mathfrak{I}_{\max}^{7n-3}|\mathcal{S}|^{2}\log^{2}(|\mathcal{S}|)}{\mu_{\min}^{4}(1-\gamma)^{8}}
, and
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{i}^{TD_{T}}
,
i=1,2,3
are universal problem independent constants. In addition, choosing
\alpha=\frac{8\log({\rm NewA}T)}{T{\varphi}_{TD}}
and
K=T/{\rm NewA}
, we have
{\mathbb{E}}[\|{\mathbf{V}}_{\hat{T}}-{\mathbf{V}}^{\pi}\|_{\infty}^{2}]\leq\epsilon
after
T=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}\epsilon}.\frac{\mathfrak{I}_{\max}^{7n-3}|\mathcal{S}|^{2}\log^{2}(|\mathcal{S}|)}{\mu_{\min}^{5}(1-\gamma)^{9}}\right)
iterations.

The proof is given in Section
C.2
in the appendix.

Note that similar to the on-policy TD-learning Algorithm
2
, off-policy TD-learning also enjoys a linear speedup while maintaining a low communication cost. In addition, this algorithm preserves the privacy of agents by keeping both the data and the sampling policy private.

6.2
Federated
Q
-learning

So far we have discussed federated policy evaluation with on and off-policy samples. Next, we will expand on federated
Q
-learning, which is presented in Algorithm
4
. We characterize the convergence of this algorithm in the following theorem.

Algorithm 4

Federated
Q
-learning

1:

Input:
Sampling policy
\pi^{i}_{b}
for
i=1,2,\dots,{\rm NewA}
, initial distribution
\xi

2:

Initialization:

{Q}^{i}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
and
S_{0}^{i}\sim\xi
for all
i

3:

for

t=0
to
T-1

do

4:

for

i=1,\dots,{\rm NewA}

do

5:

Sample
A_{t}^{i}\sim\pi_{b}^{i}(\cdot|S_{t}^{i}),S_{t+1}^{i}\sim{\mathcal{P}}(\cdot|S_{t}^{i},A_{t}^{i})

6:

\displaystyle{Q}_{t+1}(s,a)=\begin{cases}{Q}^{i}_{t}(S^{i}_{t},A^{i}_{t})+\alpha\left(\mathcal{R}(S^{i}_{t},A^{i}_{t})+\gamma\max_{a}{Q}^{i}_{t}(S^{i}_{t+1},a)-{Q}^{i}_{t}(S^{i}_{t},A^{i}_{t})\right)&\text{ if }(s,a)=(S^{i}_{t},A^{i}_{t})\\
{Q}^{i}_{t}(s,a)&\text{ o.w.}\end{cases}

7:

end

for

8:

if

t+1
mod
K=0

then

9:

Q^{i}_{t+1}\leftarrow\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{j=1}^{\rm NewA}Q^{j}_{t+1},\forall\ i\in[{\rm NewA}]

10:

end

if

11:

end

for

12:

Sample:

\hat{T}\sim q_{T}^{c_{Q}}

13:

Return:
\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}Q^{i}_{\hat{T}}

Theorem 6.2
.

Consider the federated
Q
-learning Algorithm
4
with
c_{Q}=1-\frac{\alpha{\varphi}_{Q}}{2}\in(0,1)
, where
{\varphi}_{Q}=1-\frac{0.5e^{1/4}(2-\mu_{\min}(1-\gamma))}{\sqrt{\sqrt{e}-1+\left(\frac{2-\mu_{\min}(1-\gamma)}{2-2\mu_{\min}(1-\gamma)}\right)^{2}}}
and we denote
\mu_{\min}=\min_{s,a,i}\mu^{\pi^{i}}(s)\pi^{i}(a|s)
. Denote
{Q}_{\hat{T}}=\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}{Q}_{\hat{T}}^{i}
. For small enough step size
\alpha
and large enough
T
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\|Q_{\hat{T}}-Q^{*}\|_{\infty}^{2}]\leq\mathcal{C}_{1}^{Q}\frac{1}{\alpha}c_{Q}^{T}+\mathcal{C}_{2}^{Q}\frac{\alpha\tau_{\alpha}^{2}}{{\rm NewA}}+\mathcal{C}_{3}^{Q}(K-1)\alpha^{2}\tau_{\alpha},

where
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{Q}=\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{1}^{Q}.\frac{1}{\mu_{\min}(1-\gamma)^{3}}
,
\mathcal{C}_{2}^{Q}=\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{2}^{Q}.\frac{|\mathcal{S}|\log^{2}(|\mathcal{S}|)}{\mu_{\min}^{2}(1-\gamma)^{4}}
,
\mathcal{C}_{3}^{Q}=\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{3}^{Q}.\frac{|\mathcal{S}|^{2}\log^{2}(|\mathcal{S}|)}{\mu_{\min}^{4}(1-\gamma)^{8}}
, and
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{i}^{Q}
,
i=1,2,3
are universal problem independent constants. In addition, choosing
\alpha=\frac{8\log({\rm NewA}T)}{T{\varphi}_{Q}}
and
K=T/{\rm NewA}
, we have
{\mathbb{E}}[\|Q_{\hat{T}}-Q^{*}\|_{\infty}^{2}]\leq\epsilon
within
T=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}\epsilon}.\frac{|\mathcal{S}|^{2}\log^{2}(|\mathcal{S}|)}{\mu_{\min}^{5}(1-\gamma)^{9}}\right)
iterations.

According to Theorem
6.2
, the federated
Q
-learning Algorithm
4
, similar to the federated off-policy TD-learning, enjoys linear speedup, communication efficiency, and privacy guarantees. We would like to emphasize that the update of
Q
-learning is non-linear. Hence, the result of Theorem
6.2
cannot be derived from Theorems
5.1
and
6.1
.

7
Off-policy Federated Reinforcement Learning with Function Approximation

In the off-policy TD-learning with the sampling policy
\pi_{b}
, the goal is to find the unique fixed point
{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}
which solves the following linear equation

\displaystyle\Phi{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}=\Pi_{\Phi}^{\pi_{b}}((\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{n}\Phi{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}).

(10)

Here,
\Pi_{\Phi}^{\pi_{b}}=\Phi(\Phi^{\top}\mathcal{K}^{\pi_{b}}\Phi)^{-1}\Phi^{\top}\mathcal{K}^{\pi_{b}}
is a linear function that projects into the subspace spanned by the matrix
\Phi
, and
\mathcal{K}^{\pi_{b}}
is a diagonal matrix, with diagonal entries equal to the stationary distribution of the behavior policy
\pi_{b}
.

In federated off-policy TD-learning with linear function approximation, the goal is to find the fixed point of the average operators of all the agents. In particular, suppose that each agent
i
has the sampling policy
\pi_{b}^{i}
. Denote the fixed point of the average operator as the vector
{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}
which solves the following equation

\displaystyle\Phi{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}=\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}\Pi_{\Phi}^{\pi_{b}^{i}}(\mathcal{T}^{\pi})^{n}\Phi\right){\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}.

(11)

Here,
\pi_{b}^{i}
is the sampling policy of agent
i
. The goal of the
{\rm NewA}
agents is to find this fixed point
{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}
jointly. Notice that the solution to the fixed point equation (
10
) depends on the behavior policy
\pi_{b}
. Hence, the solution to the joint fixed point equation (
11
) is different from the solution corresponding to each agent. This is the source of heterogeneity in the off-policy TD-learning with linear function approximation, which makes this problem more challenging than the prior results in this paper.

Algorithm
5
represents federated off-policy TD-learning with linear function approximation, and Theorem
7.1
states the convergence of this algorithm.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
{\mathbf{v}}_{\hat{T}}=\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}{\mathbf{v}}^{i}_{\hat{T}}
denote the average of the parameters across agents at the random time
\hat{T}
. For a small enough step size
\alpha
, large enough
n
, and
T\geq\mathcal{O}(\log\frac{1}{\alpha})
, there exists a constant
c_{TDLO}\in(0,1)
, such that we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\|{\mathbf{v}}_{\hat{T}}-{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}\|_{2}^{2}]\leq\mathcal{C}_{1}^{TDLO}\frac{1}{\alpha}c_{TDLO}^{T-2\tau_{\alpha}+1}+\mathcal{C}_{2}^{TDLO}\frac{\alpha\tau_{\alpha}^{2}}{{\rm NewA}}+\mathcal{C}_{3}^{TDLO}(K-1)\alpha^{2}\tau_{\alpha}+\mathcal{C}_{4}^{TDLO}(K-1)^{2}\varepsilon\alpha^{2}\tau_{\alpha}+\mathcal{C}_{5}^{TDLO}\alpha^{3}\tau_{\alpha}^{2},

where
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{TDLO}
,
i=0,1,2,3,4,5
are problem dependent constants, and
\tau_{\alpha}=\mathcal{O}(\log(1/\alpha))
. Choosing
\alpha=\mathcal{O}(\frac{\log({\rm NewA}T)}{T})
and
K=\sqrt{T}/{\rm NewA}
, we achieve
{\mathbb{E}}[\|{\mathbf{v}}_{\hat{T}}-{\mathbf{v}}^{\pi}\|_{2}^{2}]\leq\epsilon
within
T=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}\epsilon}\right)
iterations. Furthermore, the communication complexity to achieve this level of accuracy is
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{N}/\sqrt{\epsilon})
.

Note that the communication complexity of Algorithm
5
depends on
\epsilon
. This is due to the heterogeneity of the agents, where to achieve a certain level of accuracy, requires the agents to communicate more frequently than the homogeneous setting.

Algorithm 5

Federated
n
-step TD (Off-policy, Function Approximation)

1:

Input:
Policy
\pi,\xi

2:

Initialization:

{\mathbf{v}}^{i}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
and
S_{0}^{i}\sim\xi
and
\{A_{l}^{i},S_{l+1}^{i}\}\sim\pi^{i}_{b}
for
0\leq l\leq n-1
and all
i

3:

for

t=0
to
T-1

do

4:

for

i=1,\dots,{\rm NewA}

do

5:

Sample
A_{t+n}^{i}\sim\pi_{b}^{i}(\cdot|S_{t+n}^{i}),S_{t+n+1}^{i}\sim{\mathcal{P}}(\cdot|S_{t+n}^{i},A_{t+n}^{i})

6:

e_{t,l}^{i}\!=\!\mathcal{R}(S_{l}^{i},A_{l}^{i})\!+\!\gamma\phi(S_{l\!+\!1}^{i})^{\top}{\mathbf{v}^{i}_{t}}\!-\!\phi(S_{l}^{i})^{\top}{\mathbf{v}^{i}_{t}}
for
l=t,\dots,t+n-1

7:

E_{t,n}^{i}=\sum_{l=t}^{t+n-1}\gamma^{l-t}\left[\Pi_{j=t}^{l}\mathfrak{I}^{(i)}(S^{i}_{j},A^{i}_{j})\right]e_{t,l}^{i}

8:

{\mathbf{v}}_{t+1}^{i}={\mathbf{v}^{i}_{t}}+\alpha\phi(S_{t}^{i})E_{t,n}^{i}

9:

end

for

10:

if

t+1
mod
K=0

then

11:

{\mathbf{v}}^{i}_{t+1}\leftarrow\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{j=1}^{\rm NewA}{\mathbf{v}}^{j}_{t+1},\forall\ i\in[{\rm NewA}]

12:

end

if

13:

end

for

14:

Sample
\hat{T}\sim q^{c_{TDLO}}_{T}

15:

Return:

\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}{\mathbf{v}}^{i}_{\hat{T}}

8
Proof Sketch

In this section, we provide an outline for the proof of Theorem
3.1
, which is the workhorse to establish the results in Theorems
5.1
,
6.1
,
6.2
, and
7.1
. We adopt the Lyapunov approach developed in
[
17
]
with a Lyapunov function
M(\cdot)
. The key idea is to obtain a recursion of the form
{\mathbb{E}}[M({\bm{\theta}}_{t+1})]\leq(1-\alpha){\mathbb{E}}[M({\bm{\theta}_{t}})]+\alpha^{2}
. This will result in a convergence bound of the form
{\mathbb{E}}[M({\bm{\theta}}_{T})]\leq(1-\alpha)^{T}M({\bm{\theta}}_{0})+\alpha
. Choosing the Lyapunov function to be a “close enough” approximation of
\|{\bm{\theta}}_{T}\|_{c}^{2}
, this implies a rate of convergence on
\|{\bm{\theta}}_{T}\|_{c}^{2}
.

In order to show linear speedup in the number of agents, one ideally expects a bound of the form
{\mathbb{E}}[M({\bm{\theta}}_{T})]\leq(1-\alpha)^{T}M({\bm{\theta}}_{0})+\alpha/{\rm NewA}
. However, we obtain the slightly looser bound,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[M({\bm{\theta}}_{T})]\leq(1-\alpha)^{T}M({\bm{\theta}}_{0})+\alpha/{\rm NewA}+\alpha^{2}.

(12)

In particular, according to (
12
),
{\mathbb{E}}[M({\bm{\theta}}_{T})]
converges geometrically fast to a ball around
0
with radius
\alpha/{\rm NewA}+\alpha^{2}
. Hence, for small enough
\alpha
, the dominant term
\alpha/{\rm NewA}
diminishes with increasing number of agents, and the higher-order term
\alpha^{2}
turns out to be negligible. In order to construct such a bound, we first obtain a recursion of the form

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[M({\bm{\theta}}_{t+1})]\leq(1-\alpha){\mathbb{E}}[M({\bm{\theta}_{t}})]+\alpha^{2}/{\rm NewA}+o(\alpha^{3}),~~t\leq T.

(13)

8.1
Illustrative Example and the Challenge

The key challenge in obtaining linear speedup is handling Markovian noise. In order to illustrate the challenge, consider a special case of general stochastic approximation in the scalar case (
d=1
), with linear updates,
{\mathbf{G}^{i}}({\bm{\theta}},{\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{i})\equiv-A\theta+\theta
for all
i
, where the matrix
A
is deterministic. In addition, the term
b^{i}({\mathbf{y}}^{i}_{t})
in the update of agent
i
is driven by Markov chains
\{{\mathbf{y}}^{i}_{t}\}_{1\leq i\leq{\rm NewA},t\geq 0}
that are independent across agents. In addition, we assume full synchronization (that is,
K=1
). Hence, in this simplified setting, the update of the average parameter can be written as,

\displaystyle\theta_{t+1}=\theta_{t}+\alpha(-A\theta_{t}+b({\mathbf{Y}}_{t})),

(14)

where
b({\mathbf{Y}}_{t})=\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}b^{i}({\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{i})
.
In the following discussion, we show that
even
in this special case, achieving a linear speedup with
{\rm NewA}
is non-trivial. Consider the one-step drift of
{\mathbb{E}}[\theta_{t}^{2}]
. We have:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\theta_{t+1}^{2}]=\underbrace{(1-\alpha A)^{2}{\mathbb{E}}[\theta_{t}^{2}]}_{E_{1}}+\underbrace{2\alpha(1-\alpha A){\mathbb{E}}[\theta_{t}b({\mathbf{Y}}_{t})]}_{E_{2}}+\underbrace{\alpha^{2}{\mathbb{E}}[b^{2}({\mathbf{Y}}_{t})]}_{E_{3}}.

Next, consider two special cases.

1.

\mathbf{\textit{b}(Y_{\textit{t}})}
i.i.d. zero mean:
In this case
E_{2}
is equal to zero. Also, for
E_{3}
we have
\alpha^{2}{\mathbb{E}}[b^{2}({\mathbf{Y}}_{t})]=\frac{\alpha^{2}}{{\rm NewA}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}{\mathbb{E}}[(b^{i}({\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{i}))^{2}]+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{{\rm NewA}^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}{\mathbb{E}}[b^{i}({\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{i})b^{j}({\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{j})]=\frac{\alpha^{2}}{{\rm NewA}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}{\mathbb{E}}[(b^{i}({\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{i}))^{2}]\leq B^{2}\frac{\alpha^{2}}{{\rm NewA}}
. Hence, we obtain the form of the recursion in (
13
), which results in linear speedup. This is essentially how we achieve linear speedup in the i.i.d. case
[
42
]
.

2.

\mathbf{\textit{b}(Y_{\textit{t}})}
Markovian and zero stationary mean:
First, linear speedup in
E_{3}
is not as straightforward as in the i.i.d. case due to the Markovian nature of the underlying noise, and we no longer have
{\mathbb{E}}[b^{i}(Y_{t})b^{j}(Y_{t})]=0
for
i\neq j
. This difficulty is easily overcome by exploiting geometric mixing of the underlying noise, which enables one to bound
E_{3}
by
O(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{{\rm NewA}}+\alpha^{3})
. However, the key difficulty is in bounding
E_{2}
because
\theta_{t}
and
b(Y_{t})
are no longer independent, and the stochastic approximation update in conjunction with the Markovian noise introduces complex dependencies between the two terms. A naive way to address this is to adopt the approach in
[
75
]
to condition at time
t-\tau
where
\tau
is the mixing time of the underlying Markov chain. Thus, we have
\theta_{t}b(Y_{t})=\theta_{t-\tau}b(Y_{t})+(\theta_{t}-\theta_{t-\tau})b(Y_{t})
. The first term can be handled by the fact that
\theta_{t-\tau}
and
b(Y_{t})
are almost independent due to the Markov chain mixing. However, the second term is problematic. In particular, following the approach in
[
75
]
, it can be shown that
|\theta_{t}-\theta_{t-\tau}|\simeq\alpha\tau
, which in turn gives a
\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{2})
bound on
E_{2}
, which results in not having any linear speedup.

Thus, establishing linear speedup even in this extremely simplified case is challenging. However, in this paper, we establish a linear speedup for the convergence of a far more general heterogeneous federated stochastic approximation setting.

The main objective of Sections
8.2
,
8.3
, and
8.4
is to ensure a tight dependency on
{\rm NewA}
for a general heterogeneous stochastic approximation. Due to the federated structure of the algorithm, we also need to deal with the synchronization error across the agents, which is done in Section
8.5
. Throughout this section, “
\lesssim
” denotes inequalities in which we ignore the constants, except the step size
\alpha
and
\tau
.

8.2
Recursion on the Smooth Approximation
M(\cdot)

In Algorithm
1
, each agent updates its own parameters
{\bm{\theta}^{i}_{t}}
at each iteration. These local parameters are averaged only once every
K
steps. A key aspect of the analysis is to work with the
virtual
sequence of average parameters
\{{\bm{\theta}_{t}}=\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}{\bm{\theta}^{i}_{t}}\}_{t\geq 0}
. Since the algorithm computes these averages only at the synchronization time instants, we shall account for the difference in parameter values across agents using synchronization error terms.
Using Step 4 in Algorithm
1
, the update of this virtual parameter sequence is given by
{\bm{\theta}}_{t+1}={\bm{\theta}_{t}}+\alpha\left(\mathbf{G}(\bm{\Theta}_{t},{\mathbf{Y}_{t}})-{\bm{\theta}_{t}}+\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}_{t}})\right)
, where
\bm{\Theta}_{t}=\left\{{\bm{\theta}}^{1}_{t},\ldots,{\bm{\theta}}^{\rm NewA}_{t}\right\}
,
{\mathbf{Y}_{t}}=\left\{{\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{\rm NewA}\right\}
,
\mathbf{G}(\bm{\Theta}_{t},{\mathbf{Y}_{t}})=\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}{\mathbf{G}^{i}}({\bm{\theta}^{i}_{t}},{\mathbf{y}^{i}_{t}})
, and
\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}_{t}})=\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}\mathbf{b}^{i}({\mathbf{y}^{i}_{t}})
.

In this paper, our aim is to show the convergence of
{\mathbb{E}}[\|{\bm{\theta}}_{t}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\|_{c}^{2}]
, but the function
\|\cdot\|_{c}^{2}
might be non-smooth. Therefore, inspired by
[
17
]
, we use
M({\bm{\theta}})=\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{x}\|_{c}^{2}+\frac{1}{2\psi}\|{\bm{\theta}}-\mathbf{x}\|_{p}^{2}\right\}
, where
\|\cdot\|_{p}
is the
p
-norm for some
p\geq 2
.
M(\cdot)
is a smooth approximation of the (possibly non-smooth) function
\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|_{c}^{2}
, with smoothness constant
\frac{p-1}{\psi}
with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{p}
norm. Furthermore,
M(\cdot)
is “close” to
\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|_{c}^{2}
and
\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|_{p}^{2}
, meaning that they can be upper/lower bounded by each other by introducing some multiplicative constants.

Using
\frac{p-1}{\psi}
-smoothness of
{M}(\cdot)
gives

\displaystyle{M}({\bm{\theta}}_{t+1})\leq{M}({\bm{\theta}_{t}})

\displaystyle+\alpha\underbrace{\left\langle\nabla{M}({\bm{\theta}_{t}}),{\bar{\mathbf{G}}}({\bm{\theta}_{t}})-{\bm{\theta}_{t}}\right\rangle}_{T_{1}:\text{ Expected update}}+\alpha\underbrace{\langle\nabla{M}({\bm{\theta}_{t}}),\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}_{t}})\rangle}_{\begin{subarray}{c}T_{2}:\text{ Due to Markovian }\\
\text{ noise in $\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}_{t}})$ }\end{subarray}}+\alpha\underbrace{\langle\nabla{M}({\bm{\theta}_{t}}),\mathbf{G}(\bm{\Theta}_{t},{\mathbf{Y}_{t}})-{\bar{\mathbf{G}}}(\bm{\Theta}_{t})\rangle}_{T_{3}:\text{ Due to Markovian noise in $\mathbf{G}(\bm{\Theta}_{t},{\mathbf{Y}_{t}})$ }}

\displaystyle+\alpha\underbrace{\langle\nabla{M}({\bm{\theta}_{t}}),{\bar{\mathbf{G}}}(\bm{\Theta}_{t})-{\bar{\mathbf{G}}}({\bm{\theta}_{t}})\rangle}_{T_{4}:\text{ Due to local updates}}+\frac{(p-1)\alpha^{2}}{2\psi}\underbrace{\left\|\mathbf{G}(\bm{\Theta}_{t},{\mathbf{Y}_{t}})-{\bm{\theta}_{t}}+\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}_{t}})\right\|_{p}^{2}}_{T_{5}:\text{ Due to noise and discretization}},

(15)

where,
{\bar{\mathbf{G}}}({\bm{\theta}})=\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}{\bar{\mathbf{G}}^{i}}({\bm{\theta}})
. Next, we bound the individual terms in (
15
).

•

Term
\mathbf{\textit{T}_{1}}
:

T_{1}
can be handled identically as in
[
18
, Lemma A.1]
yielding
T_{1}\lesssim-M({\bm{\theta}_{t}})
, which is the source of the negative drift. This negative drift is achieved using the contraction property of the operators
{\bar{\mathbf{G}}^{i}}(\cdot)
,
i=1,\dots,{\rm NewA}
(Assumption
3.2
).

Starting from the term
T_{2}
, the analysis diverts from the one in the previous work
[
18
]
.

•

Term
\mathbf{\textit{T}_{2}}
:
Suppose
{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}
was i.i.d. noise. In that case
{\mathbb{E}}[\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}_{t}})]=\mathbf{0}
, which leads to
{\mathbb{E}}[T_{2}]=0
. Since
{\mathbf{Y}_{t}}
is Markovian instead, by Assumption
3.1
,
\|{\mathbb{E}}[\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}_{t}})]\|_{c}
approaches
0
exponentially fast. Now, suppose that
\mathbf{\textit{T}_{2}}
instead has the following term
\left\langle\nabla{M}({\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}),\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}_{t}})\right\rangle
. By taking the conditional expectation given the time
t-\tau
, denoted by
{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}[\cdot]
, for large enough
\tau
(such that
\rho^{\tau}=\alpha^{2}
), and using Assumption
3.1
, we have
{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}[\left\langle\nabla{M}({\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}),\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}_{t}})\right\rangle]\lesssim\rho^{\tau}\left\|\nabla{M}({\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau})\right\|_{p}^{\star}\lesssim{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}[\alpha\left\|{\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}-{\bm{\theta}}_{t}\right\|_{c}+\alpha^{4}+M({\bm{\theta}_{t}})]
. Therefore, we add and subtract
\nabla{M}({\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau})
in
T_{2}
, to get

\displaystyle T_{2}\hskip-2.84526pt

\displaystyle=\hskip-2.84526pt\left\langle\nabla{M}({\bm{\theta}}_{t})-\nabla{M}({\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau})+\nabla{M}({\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}),\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}_{t}})\right\rangle\hskip-1.42262pt\leq\hskip-1.42262pt\frac{1}{2\alpha}\|{\bm{\theta}_{t}}-{\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}\|_{c}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\|b({\mathbf{Y}_{t}})\|_{c}^{2}+\left\langle\nabla{M}({\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}),\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}_{t}})\right\rangle.

Substituting the bound on
{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\left\langle\nabla{M}({\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}),\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}_{t}})\right\rangle
in the inequality above, we get
{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}[T_{2}]
. Eventually, we shall use the tower property of expectation to get
\mathbb{E}\left[T_{2}\right]
.

•

Term
\mathbf{\textit{T}_{3}}
:

Again, if the noise
{\mathbf{Y}_{t}}
is i.i.d.,
{\mathbb{E}}[T_{3}]=0
. However, in the presence of Markov noise, similar to
T_{2}
, we need to condition w.r.t. time
t-\tau
. Further, due to mismatch of parameters across agents
\bm{\Theta}_{t}
, we also get synchronization error terms. We have

\displaystyle T_{3}=

\displaystyle\underbrace{\langle\nabla{M}({\bm{\theta}_{t}})-\nabla{M}({\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}),\mathbf{G}(\bm{\Theta}_{t},{\mathbf{Y}_{t}})-{\bar{\mathbf{G}}}(\bm{\Theta}_{t})\rangle}_{T_{31}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\langle\nabla{M}({\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}),\mathbf{G}(\bm{\Theta}_{t},{\mathbf{Y}_{t}})-\mathbf{G}(\bm{\Theta}_{t-\tau},{\mathbf{Y}_{t}})+{\bar{\mathbf{G}}}(\bm{\Theta}_{t-\tau})-{\bar{\mathbf{G}}}(\bm{\Theta}_{t})\rangle}_{T_{32}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\langle\nabla{M}({\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}),\mathbf{G}(\bm{\Theta}_{t-\tau},{\mathbf{Y}_{t}})-{\bar{\mathbf{G}}}(\bm{\Theta}_{t-\tau})\rangle}_{T_{33}}.

We can bound the first two terms as follows:
T_{31}\lesssim\|{\bm{\theta}_{t}}-{\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}\|_{c}^{2}+M({\bm{\theta}_{t}})+\Omega_{t}
and
T_{32}\lesssim\|{\bm{\theta}_{t}}-{\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}\|_{c}^{2}+M({\bm{\theta}_{t}})+\Omega_{t}+\Omega_{t-\tau}
, where
\Omega_{t},\Omega_{t-\tau}
are the synchronization errors, defined as
\Omega_{t}\triangleq\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}\sum_{i=1}^{\rm NewA}\|{\bm{\theta}_{t}}-{\bm{\theta}^{i}_{t}}\|_{c}^{2}
and is bounded in Section
8.5
. All the terms in
T_{33}
other than
{\mathbf{Y}_{t}}
depend on time
t-\tau
. Therefore, similar to
T_{2}
,
T_{33}
can be bounded using the mixing property of
{\mathbf{Y}_{t}}
. In particular,
{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}[T_{33}]\lesssim\alpha{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}[M({\bm{\theta}}_{t})+\left\|{\bm{\theta}}_{t}-{\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}\right\|_{c}^{2}+\Omega_{t-\tau}]
.
Combining these three bounds, we get
{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}[T_{3}]\lesssim{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\left[M({\bm{\theta}_{t}})+\left\|{\bm{\theta}_{t}}-{\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}\right\|_{c}^{2}+\Omega_{t}+\Omega_{t-\tau}\right]
.

•

Term
\mathbf{\textit{T}_{4}}
:

T_{4}
is due to the mismatch arising due to multiple local updates. With perfect synchronization (
K=1
), we have
T_{4}=0
. More generally, we show that
T_{4}\lesssim M({\bm{\theta}_{t}})+\Omega_{t}
.

•

Term
\mathbf{\textit{T}_{5}}
:
Using Assumption
3.3
, we can bound
T_{5}\lesssim M({\bm{\theta}_{t}})+\Omega_{t}+\left\|\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}_{t}})\right\|_{c}^{2}
.

Substituting the above bounds on
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4},T_{5}
back into (
15
), we get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\left[M({\bm{\theta}}_{t+1})\right]\lesssim

\displaystyle(1-\alpha){\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\left[M({\bm{\theta}}_{t})\right]+\alpha^{4}+\underbrace{{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\left[\|{\bm{\theta}}_{t}-{\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}\|_{c}^{2}\right]+\alpha^{2}{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\left[\|{\bm{\theta}}_{t}-{\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}\|_{c}\right]}_{I_{1}}

(16)

\displaystyle+\alpha^{2}{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\|\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}_{t}})\|_{c}^{2}+\alpha{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\left[\Omega_{t}+\Omega_{t-\tau}\right].

First, we discuss a few special cases of (
16
) under different assumptions.

1.

Perfect synchronization
(K=1)
with i.i.d. noise: since
{\Omega_{t}}=0
for all
t
,
\tau=0
, the bound reduces to the form we get for centralized systems with i.i.d. noise
[
66
]
.

2.

Infrequent synchronization
(K>1)
with i.i.d. noise:
\tau=0
, and the bound reduces to the form we get for federated stochastic optimization with i.i.d. noise
[
39
]
.

3.

Perfect synchronization
(K=1)
with Markov noise: Since
{\Omega_{t}}=0
for all
t
, the bound in (
16
) generalizes the results in
[
75
]
, which considers the linear stochastic approximation case. The bound also resembles the corresponding bound in
[
18
]
.

The last two terms in (
16
) correspond to average of Markov noise in the
{\rm NewA}
agents, and the synchronization error. These are bounded in Sections
8.4
and
8.5
, respectively. But first, in the next section we bound
I_{1}
. We would like to emphasize that bounding
I_{1}
carefully to eventually create the terms
o(\alpha^{3})
and
\alpha^{2}/{\rm NewA}
, is essential to obtain a recursion similar to (
13
). This is one of the important parts that separates our work from the previous results.

8.3
Bounding
I_{1}

In this section, we bound
\|{\bm{\theta}}_{t}-{\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}\|_{c}^{2}
in terms of
\alpha^{2}\sum_{l=t-\tau}^{t}\|\mathbf{b}(\mathbf{Y}_{l})\|_{c}^{2}
and
\alpha^{2}\sum_{l=t-\tau}^{t}\Omega_{l}
.
The bound on
\|{\bm{\theta}}_{t}-{\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}\|_{c}
follows analogously. First, using triangle inequality and the update of Algorithm
1
, at each time
t

\displaystyle\|{\bm{\theta}}_{t}-{\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}\|_{c}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\tau\sum_{l=t-\tau}^{t-1}\|{\bm{\theta}}_{l+1}-{\bm{\theta}}_{l}\|_{c}^{2}\lesssim\tau\alpha^{2}\sum_{l=t-\tau}^{t-1}[\|{\bm{\theta}}_{l}\|_{c}^{2}+\|\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}}_{l})\|_{c}^{2}+\Omega_{l}].

(17)

Among the terms above, we need to further bound
\sum_{l=t-\tau}^{t-1}\|{\bm{\theta}}_{l}\|_{c}^{2}
. It can be shown that
\|{\bm{\theta}}_{l}\|_{c}^{2}\lesssim\|{\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}\|_{c}^{2}+\alpha\sum_{k=t-\tau}^{l-1}\|\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}}_{k})\|_{c}^{2}+\alpha\sum_{k=t-\tau}^{l-1}\Omega_{k}
. This bound can be explained as follows. Consider
{\bm{\theta}}_{l}
for
l=t-\tau,t-\tau+1\dots,t-1
. Clearly, at
l=t-\tau
,
\|{\bm{\theta}}_{l}\|_{c}^{2}\leq\|{\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}\|_{c}^{2}
. Next, due to the update in Step 4 of Algorithm
1
, starting at time
t-\tau
,
{\bm{\theta}}_{l}
depends on
\{\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}}_{k})\}_{k=t-\tau}^{l-1}
, which explains the
\alpha\sum_{k=t-\tau}^{l-1}\|\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}}_{k})\|_{c}^{2}
term. In addition, the discrepancy between agents explains the term
\alpha\sum_{k=t-\tau}^{l-1}\Omega_{k}
.
Since
l\leq t-1
, we can bound (
17
) as

\|{\bm{\theta}}_{t}-{\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}\|_{c}^{2}\lesssim\tau^{2}\alpha^{2}\|{\bm{\theta}}_{t-\tau}\|_{c}^{2}+\tau\alpha^{2}\sum_{l=t-\tau}^{t-1}[\|\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}}_{l})\|_{c}^{2}+\Omega_{l}].

Substituting this bound in (
16
), we get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\left[M({\bm{\theta}}_{t+1})\right]\lesssim

\displaystyle(1-\alpha){\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\left[M({\bm{\theta}}_{t})\right]+\alpha^{3}+\underbrace{\alpha^{2}\sum_{l=t-\tau}^{t-1}{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}[\|\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}}_{l})\|_{c}^{2}]}_{I_{2}}+\alpha\sum_{l=t-\tau}^{t}{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}[\Omega_{l}].

(18)

Next, we bound the term
\sum_{l=t-\tau}^{t}{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}[\|\mathbf{b}(\mathbf{Y}_{l})\|_{c}^{2}]
to create the form
\alpha^{3}+\alpha^{2}/{\rm NewA}
promised in (
13
).

8.4
Bounding
I_{2}

If
\{{\mathbf{y}}_{l}^{i}\}
is i.i.d. noise across agents
i=1,2,\dots,{\rm NewA}
, it is easy to see that
{\mathbb{E}}[\|\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}}_{l})\|_{c}^{2}]\lesssim\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}
. This leads to linear speedup in federated learning with i.i.d. noise
[
57
]
. However, in the presence of Markovian noise, the analysis is more involved.
A crude method to bound
I_{2}
would be to use Assumption
3.3
to obtain
\|\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}}_{l})\|_{c}^{2}\leq B^{2}
, which results in
I_{2}\lesssim\alpha^{2}

[
75
]
. However, compared to the form in (
13
), this bound is not sufficient to ensure linear speedup in
{\rm NewA}
.
Hence, we exploit conditional expectation to get a tighter dependence on
\alpha
and
{\rm NewA}
. In particular, for
r\leq t
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{t-r}[\|\mathbf{b}({\mathbf{Y}}_{l})\|_{c}^{2}]\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\rm NewA}}+\rho^{2(l-t+r)},\qquad\forall l\in\{t-r,\ldots,t-1\}.

(19)

In (
19
), the first term is similar to the i.i.d. noise case, while the second term appears due to the Markovian noise. Now consider
r=\tau
. Putting in (
19
), we get
I_{2}\lesssim\frac{\alpha^{2}}{{\rm NewA}}+\alpha^{2}
. However, the term
\alpha^{2}
is too loose to guarantee linear speedup (again, recall (
13
)). Therefore, we need to go further back into the past. Next, consider
r=2\tau
. Then using the assumption
\rho^{\tau}=\alpha^{2}
, it is easy to show that
I_{2}\lesssim\frac{\alpha^{2}}{{\rm NewA}}+\alpha^{3}
, which is sufficient for linear speedup. Here we would like to emphasize that taking a conditional expectation conditioned on time
t-2\tau
is essential to achieve this bound.

Substituting the bound in (
18
) and using tower property of expectation
{\mathbb{E}}_{t-2\tau}\left[{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\left[\cdot\right]\right]={\mathbb{E}}_{t-2\tau}\left[\cdot\right]
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{t-2\tau}\left[M({\bm{\theta}}_{t+1})\right]\lesssim

\displaystyle(1-\alpha){\mathbb{E}}_{t-2\tau}\left[M({\bm{\theta}}_{t})\right]+\alpha^{2}/{\rm NewA}+\alpha^{3}+\alpha\sum_{l=t-\tau}^{t}{\mathbb{E}}_{t-2\tau}[\Omega_{l}].

(20)

Next, we discuss how to handle the synchronization error
\{\Omega_{l}\}
by taking a weighted sum of (
20
).

8.5
Weighted Consensus Error

It can be shown that the expectation of the synchronization error at time
t
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[{\Omega_{t}}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\alpha^{2}(t-s_{t}K)+\alpha^{2}(t-s_{t}K)\sum_{t^{\prime}=s_{t}K}^{t-1}\mathbb{E}[M({\bm{\theta}}_{t^{\prime}})].

(21)

where
s_{t}K
is the last time before
t
when synchronization occurred. Intuitively, the synchronization error builds up over time, starting from zero at
s_{t}K
, and depends on all iterations encountered along the trajectory, i.e.,
\{{\bm{\theta}}_{t^{\prime}}\}_{t^{\prime}=s_{t}K}^{t-1}
.
Next, we define
w_{t}=1/(1-\alpha)^{t}
. We can show that a weighted sum of the inequality (
21
) satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{W_{T}}\sum_{t=2\tau}^{T}w_{t}\left[\sum_{\ell=t-\tau}^{t}\mathbb{E}[\Omega_{\ell}]\right]\lesssim\alpha^{2}(1+\varepsilon(K-1))(K-1)+\frac{1}{2W_{T}}\sum_{t=0}^{T}w_{t}\mathbb{E}[M({\bm{\theta}}_{t})],

(22)

where
W_{T}=\sum_{t=0}^{T}w_{t}
. Note that throughout the proof, the first place where the effect of heterogeneity appears is in the analysis of the consensus error in (
22
).

Finally, by sampling
\hat{T}
according to
P(\hat{T}=t)=\frac{w_{t}}{\sum_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T}w_{t}}
independently of all other randomness in the problem, we have
{\mathbb{E}}[M({\bm{\theta}}_{\hat{T}})]=\frac{1}{W_{T}}\sum_{t=0}^{T}w_{t}\mathbb{E}[M({\bm{\theta}}_{t})]
. Weighting both sides of (
20
) with
w_{t}/W_{T}
and summing up, we get the result in Theorem
3.1
.

Due to the dependence of the synchronization error
{\Omega_{t}}
on the previous trajectory through
\{M({\bm{\theta}}_{t^{\prime}})\}_{t^{\prime}=s_{t}K}^{t-1}
, the recursion in (
20
) is not as “clean” as the recursion in (
13
). Therefore, we rely on the weighted sum technique described above and show convergence for the weighted sum of iterates
{\bm{\theta}}_{\hat{T}}
, rather than the last iterate
{\bm{\theta}}_{T}
. Similar bounds for weighted iterates have also been shown in the much simpler i.i.d. noise setting
[
76
,
42
]
.

9
Conclusion

In this paper, we analyzed the convergence of general heterogeneous federated stochastic approximation algorithms under Markovian noise (FeGSAM), where
{\rm NewA}
agents seek to find the fixed point of an operator. Our results demonstrate that the algorithm achieves linear speedup. However, in a heterogeneous setting, achieving an accuracy level of
\epsilon
requires more communication compared to the homogeneous case. Specifically, the communication complexity is
\mathcal{O}({\rm NewA})
in the homogeneous setting and
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{N}/\sqrt{\epsilon})
in the heterogeneous setting.

Based on these findings, we explored the convergence of federated reinforcement learning algorithms, where
{\rm NewA}
